#7398. Visibility Gets You On The List, Evidence Helps You Stay There via @sejournal, @billhunt

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

AI visibility can hide evidence gaps that weaken buyer recommendations as requirements get specific, making decision readiness a crucial GEO metric.

For much of the past two years, the AI search conversation has focused on visibility. Teams want to know whether their brand is mentioned, where it appears in recommendations, how often competitors surface, and whether they are being cited across ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude, Perplexity, Google, and other AI surfaces.

Those are useful metrics, but I have increasingly questioned whether they tell us enough about the corresponding business outcome.

Nearly two years ago, I began using the term “eligibility gates” to describe the detailed decision criteria that determine whether a company or product remains qualified as a buyer moves through the decision process. I later expanded on that idea when I wrote Decision Coverage: whether an organization has exposed enough information and evidence for AI to evaluate, compare, qualify, and confidently recommend.

That led to two related arguments tied to previous articles. In my article Beyond Content Parity, I argued that using tools and processes that simply match competitor content may create parity without creating any decision advantage. In Beyond Brand Sovereignty, I argued that the knowledge AI needs often exists beyond the marketing site, scattered across product, sales, support, engineering, legal, customer success, and external sources.

A recent project allowed me to test those ideas against an actual business problem.

The company did not have an AI visibility problem. It frequently appeared in AI-generated consideration sets. The concern was what happened afterward, when prospective buyers continued the conversation and began asking more specific questions about requirements, workflows, integrations, outcomes, evidence, and competitive differences.

The question was not “How do we get seen in AI?” It was, “Why, with all this visibility, is our lead pipeline shrinking?” The reality we found was that as AI took a prospective buyer further down the consideration set, they became less competitive.

To prove my thesis and identify the problem, we created 38 detailed, criteria-based prompts across several product categories and buyer segments, and evaluated them on five AI surfaces, yielding 190 responses.

These were intentionally different from broad “best vendor” prompts. They reflected the kinds of requirements buyers use to narrow a field: workflow fit, integrations, regulatory requirements, interoperability, operational conditions, analytics, platform breadth, and other decision criteria. I had to create a detailed explanation of their purpose as diagnostic-only because the brand and their SEO agency argued that no human would ever use that complex a prompt. We actually found that some prompts were even longer after talking to actual procurement managers.

The initial numbers looked excellent – even better than their current visibility scorecard. Our research found the company was mentioned in 98.4% of evaluated responses and appeared as either a primary recommendation or shortlisted option 93.2% of the time. For many GEO programs, that would be the headline of the week and champagne for everyone. The brand was almost always visible and almost always treated as a serious option. But visibility was not the problem we were investigating.

When we evaluated those same responses against the decision criteria in the prompts, 73.7% had weak or missing evidence supporting important claims or recommendations about the company. In 15.8% of runs, an important association, decision criterion, competitive distinction, or discovery signal contained what we classified as a Significant or Critical Gap. The more we refined our evidence-scoring criteria, the higher this number rose.

The critical finding was that AI systems generally knew the company belonged in the conversation. However, they were far less consistent at substantiating why it should remain there once the buyer began applying specific eligibility criteria. That is the distinction that matters and explains their declining lead pipeline.

There is a meaningful difference between asking which vendors operate in a market and asking which vendors can satisfy a specific combination of technical, operational, regulatory, and business requirements.

A model may know a company is a legitimate vendor, understand its main products, and have enough general information to put it on a shortlist. But once the buyer asks how an integration works, whether a workflow is supported, what happens under a particular operating condition, what limitations apply, or what evidence supports an outcome claim, the information requirements become more demanding. Those are not merely additional keywords. They are evidence requirements.

That explains how a brand can achieve a 98.4% mention rate and 93.2% primary-or-shortlist rate while still having weak or missing evidence in nearly three-quarters of the responses. The numbers are not contradictory. They measure different stages of the decision process.

No one can dispute that AI visibility gets the company into the consideration set. Decision readiness determines whether AI has enough information to keep it there.

See also: B2B Buyers Choose A Vendor Before They Reach Out – 3 Ways To Be Visible When It Counts

One of the more important findings was that weak evidence did not necessarily mean the company lacked the capability. In many cases, the capability appeared to exist, but the available information did not explain or substantiate it well enough to satisfy the AI surface’s confidence level.

A marketing bullet might claim an end-to-end process without ever showing the actual process, connections, or how the data flows between systems. We found efficiency claims but did not have any “proof” from internal tests or customer testimonials of the efficiency gain.

Sometimes the information was simply fragmented. A model might need to connect a product page to an integration page, then to another interoperability page, before it could infer how the pieces satisfied the buyer’s requirement. While an AI system could or should be able to make those multi-part connections, each additional inference creates another opportunity for incomplete representation or for a competitor with clearer evidence to become the safer recommendation.

We also found that the strongest content was not necessarily the longest or most buzzword-integrated, but rather content that made the relationships among capability, workflow, buyer requirements, and outcome explicit.

An interesting finding was that only first-party evidence was not inherently a weakness. Many claims lacked third-party validation, but this was not necessarily flagged as a defect. We found that authoritative product documentation may be the best evidence that a feature or integration exists. However, every claim involving superiority, effectiveness, adoption, scale, measurable outcomes, or competitive differentiation did benefit from independent corroboration by customers, industry, government, analysts, or other parties.

That means some evidence gaps are not content-writing problems at all, but does anyone else agree with the evidence?

See also: Schema, LLMs & The Low Bar For ‘Evidence’ In GEO

The findings reinforced the concern I raised in my earlier work about how easy-button AI content-generation systems create “me-too content parity.” As I explained to this brand, most of the GEO efforts to date were what I would call SEO-adjacent GEO. It is a newly rebranded SEO process that leverages AI speed for traditional content-gap analysis. Teams look at competitor coverage, People Also Ask questions, common prompts, and pages receiving citations, then create content to close those gaps. There is nothing inherently wrong with that. In this case, it appears to have worked extremely well at establishing this company’s category presence. The limitation appears when everyone says roughly the same thing, and you don’t offer details.

If every vendor claims to be end-to-end, highly integrated, AI-enabled, scalable, flexible, and focused on efficiency, those statements may help establish that each belongs in the category. They do much less when the model has to determine what “end-to-end” covers, what “integration” connects, what the AI actually does, or what evidence supports the efficiency claim.

Parity can help a company qualify for the conversation without providing enough information to survive deeper evaluation.

See also: AI Gives You The Vocabulary. It Doesn’t Give You The Expertise

Running the prompts was relatively straightforward. There wasn’t a tool or off-the-shelf framework for what we were trying to measure. We needed to build a multi-variable evidence scorecard capable of determining whether the model had enough support to evaluate the company against the criteria that actually mattered in the buying decision.

That meant defining what counted as strong evidence, what first-party documentation was enough, and when independent corroboration became important. The bigger challenge was thinking beyond the “we are great” organizational mindset to determine when an evidence gap or omission was serious enough to affect consideration or selection. That turned out to be even harder than getting SEOs to agree on ranking factors.

Those judgments then had to be translated into a repeatable scoring system and applied consistently across the prompts and five AI surfaces. It also required conversations beyond content. Sales had to help identify criteria and objections that affect deals. Product had to validate capabilities and limitations. Customer-facing teams helped identify real outcomes. In some cases, customers themselves were the best source for understanding what evidence mattered.

This is why I do not believe there will be one universal GEO evidence scorecard that every organization can download. The methodology can be reusable if you can honestly define the decision criteria and what constitutes sufficient evidence, then evaluate whether AI can connect the brand to those criteria.

As we evaluated eligibility criteria and evidence requirements, many findings no longer looked like conventional content gaps. Looking at this massive evidence gap matrix as one manager exclaimed, “that no human will ever read,” it was obvious this would become a team effort to make the changes. Content teams cannot make up content, nor will they necessarily know how everything works.

Some of the knowledge required to close these gaps is organizational in nature. We had to pry it out of product, engineering, and sales, and then run it through marketing and compliance. In some cases, we had to loop in external and partner relations when stronger third-party or attributable proof was required beyond what we could create.

The content team still has a critical role: organize that knowledge, make relationships explicit, create coherent evidence paths, and expose information in ways humans and AI systems can understand.

Most importantly, this project validated and reinforced my belief that measuring only the first AI response is insufficient. True, those broad PAA-like prompts are valuable for generating a list of options worth considering. The problem is the current tools cannot maintain state like a human workflow and aggregate the sequence of prompt turns most users leverage. Each subsequent prompt digs deeper into the criteria for consideration, maintaining preferences, and memory, and culminating in the multivariate result that I affectionately call prompt math.

This means that GEO measurement needs to become more sophisticated and account for both visibility and decision readiness. Many tools now have answer accuracy monitoring, which is a step forward. I argue that we need to put more effort into the element that is much closer to the business problem organizations should actually care about.

There is an obvious danger here because once marketers conclude that AI systems need evidence, we will probably see an explosion of “evidence-shaped content”: longer pages, superficial diagrams, weak surveys, synthetic research, and AI-generated case studies created primarily because someone believes those formats will influence an LLM, just like the explosion of Reddit submission services. That misses the point. The objective is not to manufacture artifacts that appear persuasive to AI, but to provide real content that reduces uncertainty and risk for the buyer.

If the information would not help a knowledgeable customer make a better decision, it probably should not exist merely to improve a GEO score.

As noted, the company had already achieved what many GEO programs would consider an amazing success: 98.4% visibility and 93.2% primary-or-shortlist consideration, yet 73.7% of those same responses had weak or missing evidence. Unfortunately, our analysis could not prove that this evidence gap caused every lost sale or every subsequent AI exclusion. What it does show is that a visibility-only measurement model would have missed the weakness we were asked to investigate.

Any company can succeed in entering the conversation but fail to offer enough credible evidence to remain qualified as the conversation becomes more specific.

#7395. TikTok tests shopping subscriptions

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company invited select users in the U.S. to join its TikTok Shop Plus program, which will cost about $10 a month after a free trial period.

TikTok is trying out another way to get more people shopping in its app with the launch of a new TikTok Shop Plus subscription option that offers discounts and deals for repeat buyers.

As reported by Modern Retail, TikTok began inviting selected U.S. users over the weekend to join its new TikTok Shop Plus program. Additional reporting from GeekSeller suggested that TikTok has been gradually rolling out the option over the last month.

As per Modern Retail: “TikTok Shop Plus costs $9.99 a month after a 30-day free trial, according to TikTok Shop. It includes perks such as expanded free shipping, exclusive discounts, coupon bundles and instant refunds.”

The offering is similar to Amazon Prime and other subscription shopping options, which are designed to increase customer loyalty through add-on benefits.

It’s TikTok’s latest effort to get more people to buy products on the platform as it works to build a bigger revenue stream through in-app shopping.

TikTok owner ByteDance saw significant success with in-stream shopping via Douyin, the version of TikTok available in China. With Douyin, ByteDance was able to successfully transition short-form video engagement into livestream shopping, which has since generated billions of dollars for the company.

According to KrAsia, Douyin generated nearly $500 billion in gross merchandise volume in 2025, with livestream commerce in particular driving a significant portion of that activity.

That’s likely why Douyin has been working to make in-stream shopping a bigger element of the TikTok experience as well. However, TikTok users have so far been less open to shopping in the app.

That may be changing over time, though. According to Marketing Maze, TikTok generated $26.2 billion GMV in the first half of 2025.

That’s still a long way from Douyin’s result, the numbers make it clear why ByteDance is so enamored with the opportunity, and why the company keeps looking for ways to get more people searching for products and making in-stream purchases.

A loyalty program could help on this front by encouraging the most active shoppers to keep buying from TikTok merchants, in the hopes that this will lead to further endorsements and promotions of TikTok’s shopping experience.

Patrick Nommensen, TikTok Shop’s president of strategic initiatives, told Modern Retail that the program is currently being tested “with an expanding group of eligible U.S. users.”

#7392. Threads adds advanced search filters

Threads announced some new search tools, including advanced filters and options that should make it easier to find relevant posts and people.

As shown in this example image, Threads users will now be able to filter their search results by:

There’s also an “exact match” option to make it easier for users to search for multi-term queries.

This is a much-needed update. Until now, searching for a post in Threads has been a major headache, with the app returning what often seems to be a random assortment of mentions, with very little sorting or relevance matching.

It’s often more useful to search for a Threads post on Google, although not all Threads are indexed by Google and many aren’t scanned in real-time, so that's not an ideal solution.

If Threads wants to be a more active platform for real-time discussion, discovery is a key part of that. As such, improving the search experience is an important update.

#7384. X adds video clipping

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The option allows users to share shorter snippets of creator content while still giving credit and allowing both posters to earn monetary rewards.

X has added another way for users to share content and ensure that original creators get credit, with the launch of its new clipping option for videos.

As shown in this example screen, now, when viewing a video on X that is 2 minutes or longer, users will be able to select “clip video” from the repost options.

With this, users can choose a specific segment of the original video to share, from 5 to 60 seconds in length (limited to 30% of the original clip). They can then re-post and add their own commentary.

The re-shared clip will include an overlay that displays the creator’s handle, while viewers of the re-shared clip will also be able to easily tap through to the full video to view the full surrounding context.

As per X: “The timestamp shows where the clip sits in the original video, such as 1:14/25:00.”

What’s more, both the creator and the sharing account will be credited with any impressions generated as a result of this process, ensuring that everyone gets an opportunity to earn from X’s Original Creator Rewards program.

This could be a valuable way to drive more video engagement and ensure that creators get more credit and exposure from re-shares of their content.

However, there are also some limitations.

At present, creators don’t have the option to turn off clipping for their videos. The only option to avoid unwanted clips is to delete the original video post, which will result in any clips generated from it also being removed from the app.

X also said that live broadcasts cannot be clipped yet.

However, the clipping tool is another means for X to build on video engagement as the platform looks to double down on what’s driving usage.

In 2023, X reported that video consumption was rapidly rising in the app, and said that it would try to capitalize on this shift with new video features.

It’s taken a couple of years, but in recent months, X has launched an updated livestream studio process, added new video overlay options and rolled out an updated video composer.

X also launched a Video Kickstart Bonus program last month, providing another means for video creators to get paid for their content.

Clipping adds yet another element, which could see more video content and related discussion flowing through X’s network.

X said that clipping is now available in both the iOS and Android versions of the app, with web access coming soon.

#7383. How performance metrics are rewriting creator rate cards

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Any creator asking for media dollars better have the reporting to back up the request. Advertisers aren’t cutting checks just on follower count and virality anymore.

To ink a creator deal, advertisers are asking for cost per lead, cost per conversion, earned media value and other performance metrics. In some cases, some brands are asking for Instagram link clicks and engaged YouTube views up front to determine if a creator is worth the ad spend.

“They want to see if there’s actual traffic being driven. We’re seeing a stronger emphasis on accountability for sales for each creator,” said Kimberly Duman, a managing partner at Glossary Artist creator management firm.

Creator campaigns are more often being judged against traditional performance marketing math: cost per conversion, cost per lead and return on ad spend.

Consider it yet another signal of the creator economy mirroring programmatic advertising.

As the tech has gotten better, creator platforms and agencies alike have spent the last year rolling out AI-powered dashboards with years of historical performance data. These souped up control panels allow brands to see a creator’s engagement rate, follower count, conversion rate and other performance stats before a deal is ever made.

Now, follower count becomes a vanity metric as brands look for metrics that prove purchasing behavior.

“Cost per lead or cost per conversion. Simple as that,” said Joseph Perello, founder and CEO of Props, a performance-based creator marketing platform, later adding, “Every client has a cost per conversion benchmark.”

Naturally, that benchmark looks different for every client. But as the tech improves, advertisers are retooling their scorecard to determine how good of a salesperson a creator is, he said.

Or as Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP, sees it: “The way that we work with a lot of our creators is to look at the performance of their content before we contract them.”

Before deal talks start, the global creator and influencer agency HYDP evaluates a creator’s content within a 30-day window, including view counts, but excludes any viral content to set reasonable expectations, Stamatiou said.

View counts has its own set of challenges. YouTube overhauled its view count in August. Rather than a count metric starting at several seconds in, a video view is registered the moment a video starts to play.

Now, advertiseres want “engaged views” — at least Blue Hour influencer agency is asking for those stats up front, Matt Higgins, svp and head of strategy at Blue Hour told Digiday.

“The bigger point is what we should focus on when finding and negotiating with creators,” Higgins said of the YouTube changes. “We negotiate around audience fit, shares, and expected performance, with views as one input.”

The brand tracks clicks and sales, but places greater emphasis on creator-brand alignment. “Great content will always win,” Glossary’s Duman said.

Mars readies for AI grocery agents by chasing LLM visibility and immersive stores as impulse buys shift. See what’s at stake.

TikTok Shop is offering the shopping service to more than 1 million people it has identified as its most loyal U.S. customers.

OpenAI is giving brands a free route into ChatGPT conversations through business agents, but keeping control of the data and recommendations.

#7379. As publishers await click data, Google’s AI visibility push complicates the opt-out decision

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Google’s latest publisher carrot? AI visibility.

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

The opt-out is a Search Console control that lets publishers exclude their content from Google’s AI features. The control follows the CMA’s first conduct requirement on Google, imposed over the summer. While the ruling was U.K.-only, Google opened it to sites worldwide on August 31. Page-level grounding controls are due by March 3, 2027.

But the controls are bundled: publishers can’t opt out of individual features. That leaves them weighing the potential visibility against the loss of control and waiting for click data to help judge the trade-off.

That click data, which Google is required to provide to U.K. publishers by Dec. 3, will give publishers a key missing piece of the puzzle. And given Google made the first requirements global, publishers hope it will provide click data globally too. Google hasn’t confirmed that.

Google has shown regulators and publishers that it is cooperating by sharing impression data: how often publisher content appears in AI features. But it has held back the click data, which would show whether those appearances actually send people to publishers’ sites. “That is the metric that will determine whether they [publishers] turn it off or not,” said Chris Dicker, CEO of Candr Media, which owns Trusted Reviews. “They’re not giving the metrics [currently] that actually mean anything, when they could have — because they’ve got those — they could have done it at the same time as impressions, but they haven’t.”

He added that “everyone is holding off” playing around with the new controls until they see the click data because they don’t want to turn off a large proportion of their activities.

Dicker expects Google to share the click data in the coming weeks, ahead of Black Friday, when publishers are unlikely to risk experimenting with opt-outs during a crucial sales period.

Meanwhile, Google has updated AI Mode and AI Overviews to show links more often and more prominently, added timely article carousels, and included links to related stories and more context around surfaced articles.

Google says these changes will help publishers better track, control and increase their content visibility. It’s also testing links from video creators’ and publishers’ names to their Search profiles, and says that its Preferred Sources — the feature that lets users pick and bookmark the specific websites and publishers they most trust — has grown sevenfold this year.

That visibility comes with a catch, though: the more valuable Google’s AI features become, the harder it may be to justify opting out.

For some publishers, that trade-off is already a live question. “We’re discussing the CMA controls and how we exercise those,” said Carly Steven, director of ecommerce and commercial editorial at the Daily Mail, at Digiday’s Media Strategies London event in October.

“We need to be so, so careful because just as we’re about to get the ability to remove articles on a URL basis, Google announces this new feature, and we have to ask ourselves: do we really not want to be there? It does still matter that we’re on the surfaces where users are. So it’s a very complicated picture.”

David Buttle, founder of publisher AI standards coalition SPUR, believes Google’s preferred approach is to provide enough referral traffic from its AI-related surfaces for publishers to agree to let Google use their content, rather than paying them each time it does so. That means it needs to continue providing some kind of value exchange. “The way they can achieve that within the context of the conduct requirement is to ensure they can deliver enough value in the form of traffic from those surfaces back to publishers, so that they can say yes voluntarily,” said Buttle.

Trying to maintain its exchange with publishers is tricky when its newest rival for information retrieval – OpenAI – isn’t constrained by the same legacy relationship. “ChatGPT doesn’t pay for content generally, and it doesn’t send any traffic out,” he added. “So Google’s approach is to try and intermediate as many clicks from Discover, which is a really important traffic source for publishers, and they’re serving summaries really aggressively in Discover, and bundling the control for that with the control for summaries everywhere else.”

For all the noise around opting out, publishers are unlikely to do so unless they can opt out in a more granular way, stressed Buttle. “What it [Google] can do, though, is play around with the interface and the product for Discover to juice the AI-intermediated referrals that come out of that to get as much traffic out of that, so if you’re a publisher, it makes that decision to opt out far harder,” he added.

For some publishers, the question is still how to assess the controls before deciding whether to use them. At The Economist Group, that process is only beginning.

“On the CMA point, we’re looking at it,” said Josh Muncke, vp of generative AI at The Economist Group on stage at the Digiday Media Strategies event earlier this month. “I don’t believe we’ve tested it yet, but we’re certainly looking at it.”

Whatever that decision is, it will ultimately hinge on two things: one is how much traffic Google sends The Economist. The other is how often its name appears in the AI answers at the top of the results, even when nobody clicks through. He added: There are second-order effects to turning these things off that aren’t just about the traffic. They’re about broader visibility in the world.”

Some of those effects are already on the table. Some publishers expect excluded sites to drop out of the Top Stories carousel Google now places inside AI Overviews and Google hasn’t said how its control treats that unit.

Reuters Digital Phil Andraos told Digiday in June that the lack of click data meant any decision could be slow and probably involve testing.

Muncke’s instinct runs the same way. He said he wouldn’t frame it as a single in-or-out call at a fixed point. “It will be a case more of thinking about where and how we can test, measure, and see the impacts, and make kind of like more structured, data-driven calls as we go,” he said.

The Professional Publishers Association (PPA) is appealing to the CMA to press Google to unbundle the controls so they apply to individual features, rather than solely site- and page-level, so that publishers can have a deeper level of control.

For example, a specific query answered by an AI Overview may replace a click a publisher would previously have received, while the same content could still be valuable in a broader, conversational AI search.

“You’re either opting out of search AI Mode, AI Overviews – basically all these different features, or none of them,” said PPA’s head of policy and Public Affairs Eilidh Wilson. “And this is something that we continue to hear is what most concerns publishers. We’ve been really wanting to land this with impact with the CMA, so we’ve agreed to do a further follow-up briefing with them on it to look at the different parts,” she added.

Zach “Mystic Zach” Hirsch has figured out how to maximize engagement around sports-betting content while staying brand safe.

Major AI companies are backing a new AI training opt-out standard proposal for publishers, but it exposes deeper tensions over AI scraping.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

#7374. Ogilvy and CAA launch partnership to clone celebrity talent for AI ads

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Body doubles are a popular subject in horror cinema. Usually, the doppelganger is the villain of the piece. But now, one of the leading gatekeepers in Hollywood is set to begin working with creative agency Ogilvy to run brand campaigns featuring entirely AI-generated celebrity appearances.

The WPP agency has inked a partnership with Creative Artists Agency (CAA) to generate AI ads featuring the actors, athletes and high-profile creators on CAA’s roster.

“We are witnessing the ‘NIL moment’ between advertising and entertainment,” said Carol Reed, global chief innovation officer at Ogilvy, referencing the Name, Image and Likeness industry that has grown around college sports influencers in the U.S. in recent years. “We’ve created a system where the biggest stars can license verified digital replicas safely… unlocking unprecedented agility for brands and assured control for talent.”

Brooklyn Nine-Nine star Terry Crews is among the actors who have opted in. Reed declined to name clients using the system, but said several campaigns were already being planned. “We are in the process of negotiating a few pilots right now that should be going live towards the end of the year,” she said.

The solution, called Echo, gives Ogilvy access to material kept in “The Vault” an in-house digital scanning facility built for CAA by tech company Veritone four years ago. Ogilvy and CAA’s partnership was a year in the making, per Reed. The studio uses 204 cameras to capture every facet of an actor, athlete or creator — from their voice to body language, facial expressions and the way they walk.

That material, which is refreshed by CAA clients every 3-5 years, is already used for de-aging or post-production fixes in films. Now, the agency’s staffers can pull it into WPP Open, the system behind the holding company’s various endeavors in creative production, agentic media buying and self-service creative tools, for internal storyboarding, mock-ups or final creative assets.

While the negotiations between actors or creators, their representatives and Ogilvy’s clients will still play out the same as always, the partnership is expected to yield more assets than classic shoots, at a faster pace, and reducing cost-per-asset by 65-75%.

The combination of digital capture and AI content generation could “fundamentally change celebrity endorsement economics,” according to Gartner analyst Nicole Greene.

David Raichman, executive creative director and global AI creative lead at Ogilvy, called the system “the next frontier of content… more of it, faster, and – for the first time – more trusted, not less.”

If that sounds outlandish, consider the campaigns launched by Coca-Cola and Snickers earlier this year featuring soccer coach and CAA client José Mourinho — or rather, his digital clone. Ogilvy’s partnership with the agency, which Reed said could stretch to include other talent companies in the future, gives it the chance to scale up such projects.

Echo isn’t Ogilvy’s first effort in this realm. Five years ago, Ogilvy used digital scans of Bollywood star Shah Rukh Khan to advertise small businesses across India during the Hindu festival of Diwali. That project served as a kind of “prototype” for Echo, according to Reed.

She said the agency expects the partnership to be particularly useful for ads featuring sports personalities. “They’re locked into training and seasons, so there’s long periods where they’re not available,” she said. “Now they can kind of engage and work throughout a season where normally they wouldn’t be available.”

AI tools have become increasingly common in agency and in-house creative production efforts, though they’re typically deployed with performance media assets in mind — ads for digital display, paid social or mobile app environments — rather than campaign materials featuring a famous face. Though Reed said the agency expects to use the system initially for social assets, Ogilvy’s partnership gives it an edge for clients looking to take advantage of AI gains across the entire spectrum of advertising production.

“Agencies need a first-mover advantage right now,” said Greene.

For CAA, it’s a chance to “grow the pie,” according to Alex Shannon, the talent agency’s head of strategic development. Stars can take on commercial work unfettered by the schedule of their day job on the pitch or in the movie studio — allowing them to work with more brands overall, but also capitalize on moments when their profile might be highest, such as the climax to a sporting season or during the launch of a film or TV show.

“Now, brands have the ability to move at the speed of culture, and athletes are able to do even more to engage fans at times when that relevancy is so critically important,” she said.

The partnership between Ogilvy and CAA includes a framework designed to govern how and when the agency’s clients can use the likeness of talent. CAA clients own their digital likenesses, and are able to pick and choose which businesses and markets they’re happy to appear in ads for or formally endorse.

“Talent always gets to see everything and approve everything at the end,” said Reed. AI advertising materials will also include a watermark that clearly marks a video or image as both AI-generated and approved by the star in question. Reed said the measure is a means of reassuring consumers that “this isn’t a deep fake”, as well as staying ahead of future AI regulation. (The European Union, for example, has mandated gen AI providers put watermarks on content produced using their tools since August.)

CAA has been aggressive in defending the value of its clients’ likenesses, signing an agreement with YouTube to remove celeb deep fakes from the video platform in 2024. But while watermarks might keep Ogilvy clear of future AI rules, they might not aid consumer comprehension.

“The whole point of talent is to scale authentic relationships and credibility,” said Greene. Yet consumers, when asked, are typically cool on the idea of AI-generated glitterati hawking brands. Nearly half (49%) of Britons have a negative view of AI-generated actors appearing in ads, according to YouGov, while 92% said AI generated content makes it harder to trust information online in general.

Reed argued the system is principally an extension of Ogilvy’s “palette”, rather than a means of flooding the web with celebrity slop. “The intention is that still there is a human craft and a human behind it,” she said, adding that the agency still expects to recommend a traditional shoot when a situation demands it. “Authenticity is one of those things that is going to be even more important,” she said.

Reed said that the partnership could mean brands find it easier to work with several lower-profile names (and skip the usual scheduling difficulty) rather than shelling out for a single movie star. “I’m hoping that you’ll see more [brands] opening up to B-tier athletes,” she said.

That use case may prove particularly important to Ogilvy. CMOs place an increasingly high importance on their agencies’ ability to manage their large rosters of creators and influencers, and to do so in a cost-effective manner. Eliminating the need for a TikTok personality or podcaster to actually be on location could add a sharp edge to the agency’s current pitch.

Mars readies for AI grocery agents by chasing LLM visibility and immersive stores as impulse buys shift. See what’s at stake.

TikTok Shop is offering the shopping service to more than 1 million people it has identified as its most loyal U.S. customers.

OpenAI is giving brands a free route into ChatGPT conversations through business agents, but keeping control of the data and recommendations.

#7373. How Lands’ End’s holiday pop-up feeds an always-on marketing strategy

Sarah Sylvester, who joined the retailer as CMO in March, digs into how a Holiday Tree Farm experience helps meet other imperatives.

Lands’ End is coming home for the holidays this year with an immersive pop-up in Chicago, the city where the Wisconsin-based lifestyle retailer was founded in 1963. The Holiday Tree Farm by Lands’ End is inspired by a line of archival copy that said that the retailer of apparel, outerwear and home furnishings provided “everything but the tree” at Christmastime.

“I just loved what that evoked, as far as being able to sum up how Lands’ End can help you for your holidays, because we literally have something for everybody… I feel like that really embodied what we were trying to accomplish,” CMO Sarah Sylvester said. “Lands’ End has done events in the past for holiday — how can you not? — but we really wanted to make sure that we were challenging everyone to think outside of the box.”

The holiday effort arrives as Lands’ End continues its turnaround efforts. The retailer and brand management firm WHP Global in April completed the creation of a joint venture that is intended to help those efforts. Lands’ End saw net revenue increase 2.7% year over year in Q2 2026 and made moves on marketing spend, customer acquisition and inventory that executives believe will better position it for the back half of the year.

The Holiday Tree Farm, open Nov. 6-8, will feature a variety of seasonally themed experiences, including a tote bag customization station, a vintage photo booth and a cozy lounge. The pop-up’s stations connect with the retailer’s key seasonal product categories — like outerwear and pajamas — so that the experience remains grounded in the brand.

The activation also includes a Holiday Drink Lodge in partnership with coffee chain La Colombe and a Sweater Swap in partnership with resale platform ThredUp that allows guests to exchange a used sweater for a new one.

Lands’ End’s prior collaborations with convenience store chain Wawa and lifestyle brand T&T Twin Talk were cited on a recent earnings call as marketing highlights that helped drive social media traffic and retail sales. But brand collaborations need more than just shared marketer interest, according to Sylvester.

“Even if you have really great brand names, if it doesn't make sense in some way, shape, or form with audience overlap or shared intent, it's hard to make it work,” the executive said. “We want to always ground it back to key product categories, either for us or them… how do you cut through the noise and do something that's fun and disruptive?”

Beyond other brands, Lands’ End is also partnering with Chicago native Lindi Presta and her Running the Runway run club for a “Miles & Monograms” activation and hosting a book reading and signing with local children’s author Lori Orlinsky. The goal of collaborating with such influencers is to help Lands’ End foster stronger local ties and extend the experience beyond Chicago.

“With these types of events, yes, you want to get broad awareness and great social content… even if you can't physically be at the pop-up in Chicago, you can still see and appreciate the content and enjoy it,” Sylvester said. “But specifically with Chicago, I love the localization aspect and how we're really trying to connect with the community.”

Lands’ End’s holiday pop-up arrives at a moment when marketers are reembracing experiential marketing after pandemic disruptions and amid a pendulum swing away from pay-to-play performance marketing toward IRL brand building. The retailer earlier this year teamed with Graduate by Hilton for a “Tote Tailgate Tour” that featured onsite experiences at three college towns.

“I started my career in experiential, so it holds a soft spot in my heart,” Sylvester said. “More than ever, people are craving things in real life and real experiences that they can participate in… There's just something nostalgic and comforting about when you can put down your phone and interact in real life. I think events and experiences are more important than ever.”

For Sylvester, who was appointed in March as Lands’ End’s first chief marketing officer in nearly a decade, experiential is a part of the brand’s always-on marketing — not just an opportunity for a few social posts and possibly PR-driven articles. That approach comes as some marketers are using experiential to help meet other needs around content creation and discoverability in the age of artificial intelligence.

“Experiences cost more than just paying for another paid social ad — how do we make that investment go as far as possible? Yes, it's about, hopefully, the thousands of people that will physically come through and interact with us, but also about creating content for holiday that we can use all season long,” the executive said.

In an increasingly fragmented consumer environment, uniting all aspects of the marketing mix — paid media, paid social, search, influencers, brand collaborations and experiences — around one core brand story is crucial, Sylvester explained.

“I feel like you need these three or four big moments a year of really being clear about who you are and what you stand for,” she said. “In marketing, you’ve got to do a little bit of all of it. It's always an ‘and.’”

#7370. Why publishers must act now to protect content from AI scrapers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Each technology wave brings the same promise: reach, relevance and a seat at the table. When the web arrived, publishers posted content to gain exposure, then spent a decade trying to claw back the revenue they gave away. When social media arrived, publishers threw open their archives to platforms that captured attention, data and advertising dollars. But each wave has left publishers with lower revenues, smaller newsrooms and a greater dependence on big tech platforms.

AI is the latest and potentially most disruptive wave. AI companies have spent four years crawling the web at industrial scale, ingesting decades of journalism to train models that answer questions directly instead of sending readers back to the source. Content that publishers built and funded is being taken without permission, compensation or attribution. AI companies have little incentive to pay while they can access publisher content for free.

Media leaders understand the risk. Yet research from digital analytics and customer engagement solutions provider Piano shows that fewer than 20% of the company’s 500 publisher customers use aggressive AI-bot blocking, while 30% use none at all. Without blocking, there’s no leverage to negotiate.

Some publishers worry that blocking AI means losing the chance to be discovered through AI. But less than 0.1% of publisher traffic comes from AI tools, according to data from Piano. Large language models keep users inside their own environments, so most readers never click through to the source. Anthropic sends back one visit for every 8,692 pages it scrapes; Perplexity, one for every 369. Until those numbers change, appearing in AI results doesn’t bring meaningful value.

Google’s pivot to AI Overviews has added to the pressure. Since AI Overviews launched in May 2024, clicks to publisher sites are down 49%. At Google I/O 2026 in May, Google unveiled a more AI-centric search experience that puts its chatbot-style AI Mode at the center of the search box, potentially pushing the traditional 10 blue links further down the page.

If organic search — currently 25% of traffic to Piano customers, according to internal benchmark data — continues to erode, the economic case for producing original content could collapse. Likewise, as long as AI companies can scrape content for free, they have no incentive to pay.

The path to compensation in the AI era runs through scarcity — and scarcity requires taking action like blocking bots and locking down content to create leverage. Then, publishers can negotiate.Here are four steps publishers can take now to create a path to compensation in the AI era.

Publishers can limit AI companies’ access to their content by declaring exclusions in robots.txt, blocking at the content delivery network (CDN) level, and locking paywalls to recognize only human visitors.

Some AI companies respect publisher rules, but many crawlers don’t. Piano’s analysis of Cloudflare data shows 70% of bot traffic is unverified. Perplexity has been documented circumventing blocking by impersonating Chrome browsers. Tavily openly admits its crawler skips a distinctive user agent specifically to dodge rules that let only Google crawl a site.

Basic bot-blocking may not be enough. Content protection ultimately means locking content for everyone, human or not, because the industry can’t reliably distinguish AI agents from human readers. One of the most secure approaches is requiring all readers to register, using server-side or CDN edge protection to block content from any unknown user.

An agentic commerce future in which AI systems pay publishers per crawl, per answer or per piece of content is real, but it’s not here yet. Platforms like TollBit and Pro Rata and standards like x402 and IAB’s Content Monetization Protocols are developing the frameworks. But no one is making meaningful money from bot marketplaces yet.

A defensive strategy won’t drive growth on its own. The same AI companies threatening publishers also enable valuable tools, including personalization at scale and AI-powered subscription monetization. The next generation of predictive systems will auto-optimize in real time, cutting manual work.

At the same time, an industry that spent 20 years unable to coordinate is starting to unify around how its content is used. The Standards for Publisher Usage Rights (SPUR) initiative is the clearest signal: 30 new members joined at WAN-IFRA’s World News Media Congress in June. The Real Simple Licensing Collective and the News Media Alliance are building similar infrastructure.

Publishers are also taking a harder line on how AI companies access their content. The Atlantic and People Inc. have put aggressive bot-blocking into practice. At the WAN-IFRA June conference, The New York Times’ publisher called on the industry to stop giving content away and demand fair terms.

Publishers should use AI where it creates value while retaining control over the content that powers these systems. The right move is the same one the best publishers made when subscriptions became necessary: be decisive, be clear about the value offered and build a business that doesn’t depend on someone else’s goodwill.The Publisher’s Content Protection Playbook from Piano covers practical guidance on blocking scrapers, how to know what content is worth before licensing it to AI companies, how to build leverage in AI licensing conversations and, how Piano protects content, creates personalized experiences and builds stronger reader relationships.

Zach “Mystic Zach” Hirsch has figured out how to maximize engagement around sports-betting content while staying brand safe.

TikTok Shop is offering the shopping service to more than 1 million people it has identified as its most loyal U.S. customers.

#7369. 5 social signals brands should act on now

Social media leaders are expected to know what is happening everywhere, all the time, and to have a point of view on what to do about it. But most social tools were built to monitor and measure. They report what happened. They rarely explain what it means.

That gap is where real opportunity sits. Across the brands we analyze each week, the most useful findings are rarely the biggest numbers. More often they are the shifts underneath them.

Here are five that marketing teams should be paying attention to.

Audiences are tiring of hyper-polished, solo "it-girl" glamour and gravitating toward content that feels like friends in a room. A candid carousel recapping a New York Fashion Week party reached nearly 740,000 accounts and drew more than 21,000 engagements, outperforming standard step-and-repeat coverage.

The action: Retire the solo interview. Try conversations and group chats where co-hosts or friends interview each other. Lightly produced and unscripted. Shift production focus from rigid scripts to creating environments that foster authentic interpersonal dynamics and natural conversational flow.

For one consumer tech brand, the most powerful emotional anchor was not a feature. It was relief from small, familiar anxieties: a dying battery, a package that might arrive while you are offline. Humorous, relatable posts about those moments drove strong reach and shares on Instagram and Facebook.

The action: Build bite-sized, highly visual scenarios that show the product solving an everyday problem. Focusing messaging on specific, common friction points rather than broad feature lists quickly builds emotional resonance and everyday relevance with your audience.

Many brands treat Stories as a place to repost the grid. But more often, short form content, like Instagram Stories, acts as a high-intent conversion powerhouse, generating massive click-through rates that can surpass follower reach.

The action: Rather than treating Stories as a secondary syndication feed, start treating them as optimized, high-velocity checkout funnels. Consider story-only offers and unique promo codes that turn intent into sales you can attribute, and design ephemeral content tailored for rapid action with clear, direct calls-to-action.

Relatable humor and conversational copywriting significantly outperform traditional feature-benefit structures in driving post engagement and click-through actions. We’re seeing meme-style copy ("My package: out for delivery / Me: I'm gonna be offline...") consistently outperforming feature-and-benefit announcements.

The action: Write the first three seconds of a Reel the way people actually post, using script-style overlays, familiar phrasing and emoji cues. Adopt native platform vernacular and visual formats to capture scroll attention and increase viewer retention.

Communities are starting to exhibit strong skepticism around highly marketed aesthetic features. For one technology brand, this was showing up in cases where customers felt basics were unaddressed: camera quality, software availability, stability. Sentiment scores alone would have missed the credibility gap.

The action: Show features in real-world moments where they are useful, not in isolated product shots. Frame product capabilities within everyday consumer workflows or challenges to demonstrate tangible value and maintain brand credibility.

Manscaped, the men's grooming brand, built a following by being very good at one kind of humor. But as the brand looked to expand into face and beard care, it needed to adapt its comedic approach to resonate beyond its below-the-waist roots.

Rather than doubling down, the team used cultural AI to find its voice by watching audience signals and how they responded to new creative. That surfaced new creator partners, including two University of Southern California student filmmakers who helped create one of the brand’s most viral pieces of content to date. It also helped validate the creative concept behind the brand's fall 2025 "Send Face Pics Instead" campaign, which contributed to Manscaped’s broader efforts to expand its reach and become the #3 brand in the U.S. Total Electric Shave Market.

All because cultural AI delivered rapid insights in a form leadership could confidently act on. The brand’s social director described it as "a quick postcard brief to make your point on why we need to act on something and to get the social teams heard."

None of these signals required more data. They required context: what is changing, why people are responding and what to do next. That’s what happens when you stop tracking the signals and start reading the room.

#7364. ‘We are looking more now’: The Trade Desk is eyeing the M&A aisle

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The Trade Desk has spent a decade as a public company buying close to nothing. On Monday night, CEO Jeff Green suggested that streak is up for review.

Asked about M&A on the company’s third quarter earnings call, the ad tech boss said the demand-side platform is combining the market for deals more closely than at any point in its history. He said: “Our philosophy on M&A has not fundamentally changed. We are always looking, and I will just transparently say we are looking more now than we historically have, partly because I just think there is a bunch of opportunity in the market that there has not been in the past.”

For a company whose deal record fits on an index card, that’s quite the admission.

The Trade Desk has announced two acquisitions since its 2016 IPO. It bought Adbrain, a London-based identity resolution company, in October 2017. Sincera, a New York ad data startup The Trade Desk had already backed through its venture arm, followed in January 2025. AdExchanger reported a third deal in between, though the target was never named. Terms of the Sincera deal weren’t disclosed either. The Trade Desk’s cash flow statement shows $4.35 million spent on a business acquisition in the first nine months of 2025, the window in which Sincera closed.

Neither announced deal set off a spending spree and Green went out of his way to keep it that way. Weeks after the Sincera news broke, he used his slot at the IAB’s Annual Leadership Meeting to squash talk of a new acquisition strategy. Sincera’s team was small, he told the room, before steering the conversation back to the tech The Trade Desk builds itself. Nearly two years on, the M&A philosophy is officially unchanged. The amount of looking is not.

“We do not ever want to lose sight of the opportunity in front of us and do not want to get distracted through M&A, but at the same time recognize that that can help accelerate us,” said Green. “We are always looking.”

What those targets are, Green declined to say. Most of his answer went on the bar that a deal would have to cleared, which he said is higher than it has ever been. In other words, anything the company bought would have to fit the turnaround already underway.

“It is also critical to take away that our standards are also higher than ever,” Green continued. “The reason why our standards are higher than ever is we have an opportunity to execute and accelerate our growth with all the changes that we have made in our business. I would never want to derail that by making our 17th priority our number one priority simply because we bought a company. By being super focused on the things that we look at and hold that acquisition standard to a very high level, we think we create the clearest opportunities ahead.

If The Trade Desk was ever going to break the habit of a lifetime, now would be the time. Revenue has gone backwards for the first time since the IPO, its guidance for the fourth quarter sits about 11% below last year’s and Amazon continues to eat into its share of the market. Green has spent the year rebuilding from the inside with updates to the buying platform, a tighter focus on measurement and a 15% workforce cut. But building alone hasn’t fixed the business fast enough and critics have spent months pointing to holes a purchase could plug, from its own identity data to retail transaction signals.

The money to fill those gaps is already in the bank. The company ended the third quarter with $1.23 billion in cash and cash equivalents, plus another $420 million in short-term investments with no debt on its balance sheet.

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

Forrester’s Wave study assessed 12 agency groups for their current offerings and strategies, and found diminishing differentiation among the largest of them.

#7363. How Ogilvy uses AI to unlock scale, protect rights for brand talent shoots

The WPP agency launched Echo in collaboration with Creative Artists Agency to meet marketers’ content needs while preserving consumer trust.

Ogilvy on Monday announced the launch of Echo, a proprietary artificial intelligence platform that allows advertisers to generate additional assets from a single production shoot, per details shared with Marketing Dive. Echo builds on a strategic partnership between Ogilvy parent WPP and Creative Artists Agency and operationalizes The CAA Vault, the talent agency’s system for capturing, storing and licensing digital likenesses of its clients.

Echo looks to solve for marketer needs around content creation while protecting talent rights and intellectual property. Traditional productions of advertising featuring celebrities and athletes — a $25 billion-dollar annual global market, per Ogilvy — increasingly do not meet the needs of always-on, digital-first campaigns. At the same time, brands and their ambassadors are working to meet consumer demands around trust in an ecosystem heavy with deepfakes and other unauthorized artificial intelligence-generated content.

“Clients want more content, and [talent agencies] have high quality assets. How do we bridge those things together?” said Carol Reed, global chief innovation officer at Ogilvy. “It's not about creating new software to sell to people. It is more about how you solve the question around human AI likeness and making it scalable and repeatable for wide-scale use.”

Echo, commercially available across the WPP network this month, is powered by WPP Open, the holding company’s AI-powered operating system that is at the heart of its modernized offering. The workflow begins with capturing voice and video as part of the CAA Vault process. Ogilvy then takes those models and creates storyboard elements and creative assets that are turned into ads, all with approval from client and talent.

The system allows for an asset shoot to generate 30 to 50 media-ready assets, rather than the eight to 10 yielded by traditional productions, reducing average cost-per-asset by an estimated 65% to 75%. But beyond cost effectiveness, investment optimization, contextual relevance and personalization, Reed wants clients to use Echo to think bigger, especially as creatives get more comfortable with AI tools.

“I don't want people to feel like we're cutting corners. It's about ideally unlocking impossible things,” she said. “Being able to have more ideas come out of it and not just variations is really exciting to me… We can land more creative ideas in impossible situations.”

Echo is designed to meet the needs of marketers, agencies and talent, but Ogilvy also validated the technology with consumers, surveying more than 1,200 respondents in the U.S., UK and India through a mix of intuitive association research methods and traditional survey methodology.

More than 80% of global respondents recognized talent-approved messaging as a cue of talent-approved AI usage — approval that strengthened ad appeal among more than 76% of global respondents. Even consumers generally skeptical of AI reported that explicit talent sign-off increased trust, credibility and reassurance.

“Trust in advertising has always come from two things: someone you believe and an idea worth believing. AI has challenged both,” David Raichman, executive creative director and global AI creative lead for The Ogilvy Group, said in a statement. “Echo puts them back together: the talent’s consent on every asset, and a creative idea strong enough to deserve it.”

Trust is built into Echo in several ways. Generated assets are automatically checked against machine-readable talent usage terms and brand safety guidelines, with assets unable to be released without final sign-off through an approval portal. AI models are trained only from CAA Vault data, and all cleared consumer-facing assets have a visible watermark in the format: “[Talent Name] Approved | Recreated with AI.”

“I actually didn't know what was going to come out of that research. I was a little nervous, to be honest with you, but I think trust is the main theme,” Reed said. “We're at a point now where people aren't afraid of AI. You see it in your feeds, and I think people are getting to the point where they appreciate disclosure and transparency.”

While Ogilvy would not reveal which advertisers or talent have used or plan to use Echo, Reed confirmed that pilots are in process.The manner in which talent, advertiser and creative agency can have conversations together, rather than separately, is a change which could require all parties to adjust.

“This technology creates this convergence of acknowledging what could be possible if we all aligned our ducks, and so that takes a little bit of time to find the right use case,” Reed said. “We never want to feel like we're using AI or technology for the sake of using it.”

#7361. Chief Customer Officer at Xapo Bank

Diversity is at the heart of who we are at Xapo Bank. We’re a fully distributed team of over 160 Xapiens that work remotely from 50+ countries around the world.

Our beginning: A world that enjoys economic freedom and wealth protection,no matter where you live or who is running your country.To achieve that, we search the world for the best people for the job. We work hard, think globally, and inspire each other to learn and grow. We are committed to changing the way things are done.

Although we are headquartered in Gibraltar, this is a full time, 100% remote position Work from anywhere!

Xapo's product suite has grown from a Bitcoin vault into a full member proposition: BTC and USD accounts that pay interest, a global card with Bitcoin cashback, Bitcoin-backed loans, a growing suite of BTC wealth and credit products, and access to stocks, ETFs, and stablecoins, all inside one regulated relationship.

Xapo is a bank, and a bank earns on the money its members choose to hold with it. Each of those products shipped well. What hasn't yet existed is a single owner of the economics of the customer relationship: one person accountable for winning the right members, growing what they hold with us, and keeping them, and for the balance-sheet products that make that possible.

The Chief Customer Officer is a new role at the top of the house, reporting to the CEO. You will run the stream that grows what members hold with Xapo, and you will own the Customer P&L, retail and institutional. It brings growth, client coverage, product management, and product design under one leader who is judged on customer contribution, the assets attracted and kept, and the members who stay. This is a customer-focused role in the most commercial sense: what a member earns from us, not the feature we shipped, is the centre of every decision.

The measure of success is a Customer P&L that grows on what members hold and keep: LTV:CAC improving, approved-to-paid rising, churn falling, and customer contribution the business can plan against.

This is a banking role, not a requirements-gathering role, a marketing role, or a role you can run from a dashboard. Products are priced on the balance sheet and judged on the money they attract and keep.

This part matters as much as the mandate. The known failure mode for this role, in any company, is a CCO with a polished customer-insight deck and no ownership of the number. That is disqualifying here.

Day 30: The Customer P&L baseline is documented across retail and institutional: customer contribution, LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, net paid-member growth, and assets attracted and kept. You have formed your own independent view of where members are won, lost, and under-served, and shared it with the CEO and Board.Day 60: The unified Customer operating model is live: one set of key results across Growth, Client Coverage, Mission PMs, and Product Design, read weekly from one source. Every mission PM owns a product's contribution and its price, and the roadmap is ranked on what it earns, built with Finance and the line leads.Day 90: At least one measurable improvement in LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, or net new AUM flow is live and attributable to a change you made, not a feature ship, an outcome moved. Every cell in your line has defined key results and an owner who explains the number every week.

Shape the FutureImprove lives through cutting-edge technology, work 100% remotely from anywhere in the world.

Great work-life balanceBuild amazing things with a balance of autonomy and collaborative teamwork. Set your own work schedule and make use of a flexible PTO plan when you need to recharge.

Expect ExcellenceCollaborate, learn, and grow with a high-performance team. Learn how you learn best - from books to conferences, you’ll get a yearly budget for your individual learning and development goals.

At Xapo, we prioritize consumer protection and adhere to regulatory requirements by ensuring that all Xapiens are accountable for upholding principles of fair treatment, transparency, and ethical conduct in their interactions with customers and stakeholders.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7360. Amid a difficult transitional year, The Trade Desk posts a rare quarterly revenue decline

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The latest batch of ad tech earnings disclosures was made public after the bell yesterday, with The Trade Desk posting a rare annual revenue decrease, only the second in its decade as a publicly listed company, and a sign of the scale of its turnaround challenges.

The industry’s largest independent demand-side platform, whose immediate rivals are Amazon and Google, recently trumpeted the return of The Trade Desk cofounder and CTO Dave Pickles to help mount its comeback after a challenging 18-to-24 months.

The DSP posted Q3 revenue of $736 million, representing a 0.5% annual dip – the last such dip occurred at the depths of business uncertainty during the Covid-19 pandemic – a sign of the scale of the turnaround challenge it faces.

Meanwhile, its ad tech cohorts posted revenue increases during the reporting period on the same day (see chart below), another sign of the sector’s mixed fortunes.

Jeff Green, cofounder and CEO, The Trade Desk, noted the company’s reorganization efforts of the last year, which included a 15% headcount cut during Q3, plus its third-party audience and agentic offerings, plus its plans for more transparent measurement for advertisers. “I’m extremely excited to have him [Pickles] more deeply engaged with the team as we move into this next chapter,” Green told analysts, “we spent the last year changing our company because the industry has changed and is continuing to change.”

Execs there pointed out that revenue performance during the period was “meaningfully ahead of the outlook we provided,” noting how video, including CTV, now accounts for more than half of its revenue mix, with the DSP also forecasting that its Q4 revenue would be “at least $755 million.”

In a prepared statement, Jeff Green said, “We are moving with greater urgency across the company,” but acknowledged, “we are still operating below the level of growth we expect from ourselves.”

Facing questions from analysts, Green later outlined the company’s planned tactical changes, as well as when it expects to return to more sustainable growth, noting how the company needs to “make the power of The Trade Desk easier to use, easier to adopt and easier to prove incremental outcomes.”

He added, “Open measurement is expected to better prove incremental outcomes… while open measurement will be implemented this quarter, we expect the product to improve our growth next year and provide a tailwind for years to come.”

Under further questioning from analysts, Green noted how The Trade Desk – a company that has been notably reticent over mergers and acquisitions – was more open than ever to M&A given the current technological landscape.

However, that shouldn’t be mistaken for a change in the company’s attitude to writing checks for more ad tech, even if it does lead to more lines of business. “Our philosophy on M&A has not fundamentally changed,” he told analysts.

“We’re looking [at M&A prospects] more now than we historically have, but that said, it’s also critical to take away that our standards are also higher than ever,” Green said, adding that his leadership would never want to “derail” its current trajectory “simply because we bought a company.”

Green’s comments came the same afternoon as Zeta Global posted revenue of $493 million, up 46% year on year, making it one of the fastest-growing companies in terms of revenue throughout the period, with the company further raising its Q4 revenue guidance to the range of $521 million to $525 million, a figure that would represent up to 33% annual growth.

Meanwhile, rival DSP Viant posted $110.4 million in Q3 revenue, a 29% annual increase, as well as the purchase of generative AI company Waymark, with management noting they expected Q4 revenue in the region of $130.5 million to $133.5 million.

Elsewhere, supply-side platform PubMatic posted revenue of $79.6 million, up 17% year on year, with company leadership keen to underline an ongoing industry trend that many feel is helping to bring more equity to the historic power imbalance between the buy- and sell-side of the industry.

“AI is accelerating the shift from sell-side targeting to sell-side decisioning, enabling buyers to act on richer intelligence closer to the inventory and first party [sic] insights,” noted Rajeev Goel, PubMatic CEO, in a prepared statement, which also forecast Q4 revenue in the range of $91 to $93 million, a prospective increase of 15%.

#7358. Pepsi’s holiday push includes bigger retail presence, soda for Santa

New ads expand on a “Food Deserves Pepsi” platform that launched in 2024 and positions the soft drink as the ideal pairing with food.

Pepsi is ramping up its marketing activity around the holiday and football seasons, two occasions with a strong connection to food, for efforts that build on a “Food Deserves Pepsi” platform that launched in 2024 and positions the soft drinks as an essential pairing with food. Other moments Pepsi has looked to plug itself into as part of the platform have included soccer viewing parties, barbecues and pizza delivery.

The TV spot will be amplified across a variety of channels and during key moments, including NBC’s holiday tree lighting programming, college football games on Christmas Eve and Christmas Day NBA and NFL games. Additionally, Pepsi expects to have 80% more visibility in retail locations over the year-ago period through holiday packaging, displays, inventory and customer-specific programming throughout the season.

A limited-edition Pepsi Vanilla & Cream Holiday Bundle will be available starting Nov. 19 on Walmart’s website and includes holiday baking items and Pepsi merchandise. Pepsi Vanilla & Cream launched Oct. 18 and has so far received a strong consumer reception, per the brand.

Tapping into football hype, Pepsi launched custom creative featuring Bettis on Nov. 4 across Walmart’s website and social channels. A video, titled “Flag on Flavor,” sees the NFL legend surprising Walmart shoppers while promoting a “Tailgating Deserves Pepsi” campaign. The effort will also be promoted across 15 NFL team partnerships via retail, new creative and in-person experiences.

Pepsi has deepened its connection to football via retail visibility, with a 30% increase in point-of-sale presence over last year, per the brand. Pepsi has been the official soft drink of the NFL for nearly three decades. Earlier this year, the brand advertised in the Super Bowl with a 30-second national spot that featured a polar bear who is befuddled after picking Pepsi Zero Sugar over Coke Zero in a blind taste test.

PepsiCo in Q3 saw net revenue increase 5.6%, per an earnings statement. For its beverages division, the company reported improving organic volume trends, driven by strength in energy drinks, functional hydration and zero-sugar and flavored soft drinks. Still, PepsiCo is looking to trim spending as its broader North American business continues to face sales challenges amid shifting consumer habits.

This fall, KFC U.S. announced that it will replace Pepsi beverages with Coca-Cola across its restaurant system by spring 2027. The win for rival Coke comes as its Mr. Pibb beverage rises as the fastest-growing brand this year. Another rival, Keurig Dr Pepper, is planning to increase its marketing investment, potentially presenting another challenge for PepsiCo.
