#7265. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.About the Role

We’re looking for a Product Manager, Administration & Governance to join our Product Team and make Supabase the default starting point for software built inside a company.

Supabase already spreads from the bottom up: someone has an idea, creates a project, and builds something useful. Inside most companies, Supabase starts the same way: someone has an idea, creates a project, and builds something useful. The next step is making that momentum company-wide: one platform where anyone can start building, and where IT can see what’s running, who owns it, and what it costs.

You’ll own how companies run Supabase as their internal development platform: approved by IT and easy for anyone in the company to use. You’ll make it possible for people and the agents they work with to start building without waiting on infrastructure, while giving platform, IT, and security teams the controls they need to say yes. The goal is to make the approved path the easiest path, from an early experiment to a production application, with the controls companies need built in.

Own the product strategy and roadmap for internal innovation, from a company’s first IT-approved rollout to adoption across teams and business units.

Make getting started self-service, whether someone creates a project in Supabase, through an internal developer portal, or with an agent. Give builders the access and infrastructure they need without a ticket for every project.

Steer scattered usage into a manageable company-wide platform. Give companies one view of everything they run on Supabase. Shape how ownership, delegated access, and boundaries work so teams can move independently as usage grows.

Partner closely with platform, IT, security, and finance teams to understand what it takes to approve Supabase and keep it approved. Translate their needs into practical identity, policy, audit, and spend controls that don’t slow builders down.

Define the path from experiment to production, including project ownership, access, and the guardrails that change as an application becomes important to the business.

Work with Engineering, Design, Sales, and Customer Success to turn early customer rollouts into a repeatable experience, rather than a new custom integration for every company.

Define what success looks like, instrument it end to end, and let the data guide what gets built next—from time to first project and self-service adoption to production usage and expansion across the company.

Have 7+ years of product management on developer tools, infrastructure, or internal developer platforms.

Have built self-service products that developers love and platform or IT teams trust. You understand both the person trying to ship an app and the team responsible for how it runs.

Ideas come from customer conversations, industry events, telemetry across more than 80,000 servers and the CPO’s strategy; the team’s job is to turn them into something a customer will pay for within a quarter, using AI coding agents throughout.

We evaluate engineers partly on how effectively they work with coding agents, and in the innovation team this is the main lever on speed. As the lead you model and coach it: prompt specificity, review quality on agent-generated code, context provision, iteration and error recovery, and end-to-end task completion. #7222. The Washington Post Nears Breakeven After ‘Hundreds and Hundreds of Millions’ in Losses

This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free, weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.

After years of steep losses, layoffs, and leadership churn, The Washington Post is within striking distance of breaking even financially, according to executive editor Matt Murray.

In a conversation at the Web Summit conference in Lisbon, Murray argued that the publisher is poised to turn a corner in 2027. While that won’t be easy, The Post has recently begun rebuilding its commercial momentum, stacking up a handful of revenue wins alongside a string of editorial scoops.

In the third quarter, direct advertising sales at The Post notched their strongest year-over-year growth since 2021, while programmatic revenue was up 48% year over year as of September, according to a company spokesperson.

Meanwhile, the newsroom is hiring again, adding James Politi from the Financial Times, Nancy Cook from Bloomberg, and Ian Ward from Politico, among other additions.

The Post is also relaunching its Ben Bradlee award, an internal designation that honors the work of an outstanding reporter. On Tuesday, the award will go to Hannah Natanson, whose home the FBI raided in January, according to Murray. The Post will also be recognizing Heidi Levine, the photojournalist who was attacked by Israeli settlers in the occupied West Bank last month.

In the Q&A below, Murray reflects on five years of turmoil at The Post, the first few months of the D’Onofrio era, and his plan to own post-Trump Washington.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

Mark Stenberg: How did election night go?

Matt Murray: We had a good night, and we wanted and needed that. This whole year has been about getting the layoffs behind us, but more than that, getting five years of turmoil behind us. The layoffs were terrible, but they were a kind of climax to a long stretch of buyouts and staff turnover. We hoped they would punctuate it, and that seems to be what happened. The rest of the year has been about stability, core coverage, and breaking scoops.

Mark: You were recruited to The Post by Will Lewis, who left in February after much controversy. How does the leadership style of his successor, Jeff D’Onofrio, stack up?

Matt: I’m cautious here. Will is a friend and somebody I’ve worked with for a long, long time, so I don’t want to make too much of a contrast. But we had some deep-seated challenges in how we were organized and how we understood our audience. We didn’t have great data. When I arrived two and a half years ago, too much of the newsroom was still oriented toward print. We had more foundational work to do than people realized. Jeff brings a low-drama, data-oriented focus on fundamentals. He’s very calm. He’s about the journalism, but he knows he’s not a journalist. He’s a business guy, and he relies on us. We haven’t had any clashes about that.

Matt: I’ll be honest. We had years of losses, and steepening losses. That’s the truth. We’re a private company and don’t share the figures, but it was hundreds and hundreds of millions of dollars. Now we’re within striking distance of break-even, and I expect next year will be our best year in seven years. We’ve done the right-sizing we needed, which was tough.

FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context

Mark: Layoffs might reduce costs, but where do you see growth coming from?

Matt: The Post has got to be about Washington, and I didn’t think we were dominant enough there in reach and comprehensiveness. We’ve had real successes this year: the ballroom, the Kennedy Center, the monuments. But how do we own post-Trump Washington? The whole city has been oriented to Trump for 10 years, and he’s still the center of the action. But last week was about millennials and Gen Z coming to the fore. I’m an older guy, and I’m trying to understand the world through their eyes. This is the first time in my lifetime that the boomers are receding and the next generations are rising. That’s the audience we want for news.

Mark: What editorial changes are you making that will help drive new business?

Matt: One thing we’re trying to do better is understand franchises. We suspended our daily podcast because costs overran the revenue, but now we can sell on the ad side, including through a partnership with Acast. The Post talked for years about [advice columnist] Carolyn Hax having a podcast. She has one now, and it’s taking off quickly. Personal finance reporter Michelle Singletary has one too. We’re reviving a version of the daily podcast, and we have at least half a dozen people for whom we can find that sweet spot of newsletter, podcast, and writing.

Mark: People poke at The Post for not acquiring properties the way The Times does, which has recently set its sights on Letterboxd. How do you see that?

Matt: The Times deserves enormous credit for how they reoriented the entire company and how it interacted with the newsroom. It took them many years. In hindsight, it seems inevitable, but it wasn’t. Our company struggled during that decade. We had successes in the more open internet age, like Facebook partnerships, and of course we had a Trump bump. We went from something like 35,000 subscriptions before Trump to 3 million. In the long run, we probably need our version of that model, with the journalism at the center. But we’re still getting the Post itself where it needs to be.

City Casting Call (EXCLUSIVE): The cofounder and chief executive of City Cast, David Plotz, is stepping away from the nationwide network of city-based podcasts after six years, according to the company. As part of the transition, the publisher is naming cofounder and former chief creative officer Andi McDaniel as its new chief executive. Last October, I wrote about the recalibration afoot at City Cast, which was shuttering certain markets, expanding others, and tweaking its editorial strategy. At the time, it was generating around $5 million but had not yet reached profitability.

Continued: The leadership change comes at an intriguing moment: City Cast is backed by Graham Holdings, which also owns Slate. In late October, Slate named its editor in chief, Hillary Frey, as its new CEO, replacing Dan Check; then, last week, it laid off a small number of staff, according to an inside source. It would appear that some end-of-year housekeeping is underway at Graham, which—after selling The Washington Post to Jeff Bezos in 2013—has struggled with some of its media holdings. In 2023, after paying $348 million for the media company Leaf Group, which itself would shutter in 2025, Graham Holdings president and CEO Timothy J. O’Shaughnessy admitted to investors that they had “overpaid” for the asset. “I screwed this one up,” he wrote in a shareholders letter at the time.

AP Up (EXCLUSIVE): The Associated Press had a big week last week. In addition to penning a content licensing agreement with Anthropic, the first of its kind for the artificial intelligence firm, the publisher also grew elections revenue by more than 80% compared to the 2022 midterms, according to the company. The AP saw its highest traffic since the 2024 election, with over 23 million pageviews, a sixfold increase over election night in 2022.

Multimedia Brew: The New York Times veteran Nina Lassam joined Morning Brew Inc. as its first senior vice president of multimedia on Wednesday, MBI chief executive Robert Dippell shared on LinkedIn on Tuesday. Lassam worked at The Times for nearly a decade, where she helped build out its now robust audio and video offerings. The hire underscores just how seriously Morning Brew Inc. is taking its podcast, social video, and creator franchise efforts.

In our conversation, Dreyfus shared a number of surprising elements, including the unlikely way in which the OpenAI deal originated, what Le Monde did with the licensing revenue it gained from the deal, and why it has avoided the fallout from Google Zero.

Mark Stenberg: Le Monde was the first French publisher to sign with OpenAI. How did that happen?

Louis Dreyfus: In fall 2023, I saw that Sam Altman was speaking at Station F, so I went. When they took questions from the room, I raised my hand and said: “I’m the CEO of Le Monde. Seventy-five percent of my revenue comes from readers. You need reliable data, and you give it to users for free. If you want Le Monde to keep publishing reliable news, we need to negotiate.” He said he knew Le Monde and would be happy to. Three months later, we had a deal.

Mark: What have the partnerships actually delivered?

Louis: First, a significant and sustainable revenue stream at a time when advertising in Western Europe is slow. Second, a new audience, as the readers click through far more than people coming from Facebook, and they convert to subscriptions. And I decided to redistribute a share of the licensing revenue from the deal to the newsroom, so every journalist at Le Monde gets an annual bonus from it.

Mark: Anthropic has largely avoided these kinds of deals. Have you spoken with them?

Louis: Our last conversation was almost a year ago. They said it was too early and that their focus was B2B, not general news. I believe that will change, though, as they prepare to go public and investors will ask whether their data is stolen or legitimate. The more surprising holdout is Mistral, a French unicorn backed by the French government that refuses to sign a licensing deal.

Mark: Google rolled out AI Overviews in France this summer. What impact have you seen?

Louis: It launched in mid-July, and we haven’t seen a drop in audience. I was in London last week, and British publishers said the same about general news. They lost traffic on evergreen content, food, automotive, and consumer electronics.

#7219. Growth Copywriter at Winona

We’ve built all of our technology in house including an EHR, patient care admin system, patient portal, website, marketing technology etc. Winona has two compounding pharmacies and a team of in house physicians providing world-class care via a fully vertically integrated business model.

Lifecycle Growth & Retention. Write, test, and optimize automated onboarding, activation, adherence, churn-save, and win-back sequences across email, SMS, and push notifications to drive LTV.

#7202. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Learn one agent deeply: system prompt, tool contracts, orchestration triggers, compliance gates, and the implementation process from kickoff to go-live

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

7+ years of software engineering in a fast-paced environment, preferably in SaaS and/or Fintech, with senior TypeScript and Node.js backend experience in a large codebase (NestJS, CQRS, schema-validated boundaries, dependency injection). We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

Security and auth judgment: OAuth and machine-to-machine auth, tenant-scoped access, secrets handling, no PII in logs. Our agents touch sensitive member data and operate under consumer-contact regulation

Customer presence: you can sit across from a VP of Collections, hear a request, and say "here is what I recommend and why", including when the answer is no. You are comfortable making calls in the room and owning them afterwards

Code-quality judgment under deadline pressure: you take on debt deliberately, name it, and pay it down, rather than gold-plating or leaving silent debt behind

Genuine appetite for LLM agents: eval discipline for non-deterministic systems, reading a system prompt like code, understanding that some decisions must never reach the model. Curiosity plus shipped work is enough; research depth is not required

Nice to have, explicitly not required: voice and telephony (LiveKit, Twilio), Temporal or event-driven orchestration, Python, core banking or LOS integration experience, credit union or banking domain knowledge

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt. Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. WiseTech Global is a leading developer and provider of innovative software solutions for the logistics, global trade and supply chain industries worldwide. We serve more than 22,000 logistics companies across 193 countries, 23 of the 25 largest global freight forwarders worldwide. With the acquisition of e2open, we have expanded our network to encompass over 500,000 connected enterprises across manufacturing, logistics, channels and distribution. With the vision to be the operating system for global trade and logistics, we are creating a multi-sided marketplace connecting carriers, logistics providers, importers, exporters and shippers.

In August 2025 WiseTech Global, developer of the world's leading logistics execution software CargoWise, completed its strategic acquisition of e2open. This acquisition represents one of the most significant strategic moves in global supply chain technology in recent years, and it creates an extraordinary moment to join this business.

WiseTech's CargoWise platform has historically focused on logistics service providers such as freight forwarders, carriers, and customs brokers. The e2open acquisition brings a deep, complementary product suite and customer base on the other side of the supply chain: the buyers, importers, exporters, shippers, manufacturers and brand owners who generate the freight in the first place.

Together, the two businesses create something that has never existed before: a truly end-to-end, multi-sided global trade and logistics marketplace connecting demand, planning, supply, channel, transportation and execution in one ecosystem, all underpinned by AI and data at scale.

We are seeking an experienced and driven Area Sales Director to join our growing US sales organization. Each Area Sales Director owns a portfolio of approximately 20-30 enterprise accounts within our CPG business unit.

#7199. Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

The Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit is the technical owner and architect of the systems that power our go-to-market engine. You'll decide how our GTM stack is designed, what we build versus buy, and where we invest next — and you'll be hands-on building it: Salesforce architecture, integrations, enrichment pipelines, and the agentic workflows that help Sales and Marketing move faster and smarter.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

Partner with the Billing Systems Engineer to automate the CPQ-to-Billing handoff.

Extensive engineering experience in GTM or business systems, including owning a GTM stack's architecture end to end — build-vs-buy calls, vendor evaluations, and systems designed for a multi-year horizon.

Deep, hands-on Salesforce expertise — you understand the data model, have built complex flows and automation, and you think about it as an engineering system, not an admin tool. This is the primary bar.

HubSpot experience in a marketing context, including the HubSpot-Salesforce sync and what breaks and why.

Technical depth beyond the UI — you've built integrations with APIs, webhooks, and event streams, and you know when a native tool (a Flow, a Clay table, a HubSpot workflow) is the right answer versus when it's time to write code.

Agentic workflow experience — you've designed and deployed AI agents that act on GTM data in production.

Strong change management practices — you've shipped changes to live systems while protecting active deals and campaigns.

Experience influencing senior stakeholders — translating business strategy into system requirements and roadmaps.

A history of mentoring engineers, from early-career to senior.

Salesforce CPQ: approvals, order forms, discounting rules, and the closed-won-to-billing path.

SQL and data modeling fluency; familiarity with a warehouse-centric stack (BigQuery, Snowflake, dbt, reverse ETL).

Experience with billing platforms (Stripe, Zuora, Maxio) and the handoffs between GTM systems and billing.

Experience managing a GTM tooling budget or vendor portfolio.

Experience as a technical lead on a GTM or business systems team.

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

Contribute to open source alongside world-class engineers
